68. WILLIAM NEWPORT and HENRY WINN were indicted for publishing a libel on Frederic Trimm.
MR. BESLEY Prosecuted; MR. GRAIN appeared for Newport.
During the trial a consultation took place, and with the consent of Counsel on both sides the Jury were discharged without giving a verdict.
[trialAccount t18841117-69]
69. CHARLES HILLS TOWNS (36) , Embezzling the sum of 1 l. 9 s. 4 d. received by him on account of Mr. Newton his master.
MR. F. FULTON Prosecuted; MR. KEITH FRITH Defended.
WALTER NEWTON . I am a draper, of 168, Trafalgar Road, East geenwich—the prisoner was my manager and first salesman on 21st October, and had been filling that position for two months—when my salesmen make a sale to a customer in business or out I provide each of them with a book which contains an invoice with my name, and that is filled in and given to the customer; they are numbered, and on the other side is a corresponding duplicate which is also filled in, and the two are torn out together and taken to the cashier at once—this is the prisoner's book ( produced); it has the name of Towns on it in his handwriting—this is the duplicate I spoke of—he has never accounted to me for the sum of 1 l. 9 s. 4 d. for cashmere sold to Mrs. Lyons on 21st October—this is one of my bills in the transaction in question—on the evening of the 21st, from information received, I sent for the police after business hours—he was not charged then; we did not know his address—the following morning he was charged with embezzling 1 l. 9 s. 4 d.—he said he was going to keep the money until he had sold this particular customer more goods, and then he intended handing it over in one large amount—I said I should give him in charge—he then went down on one knee, and put his hands in a praying position, and asked me to forgive him, as he would be a ruined man—this paper, No. 39, was brought to me after business on
Monday evening by Heath, all torn in pieces, and I put it together as it is now—I asked the prisoner before the constable to account for the 39 cheque which was missing, and he said, as I have told you, that he was going to keep the money until he had sold these customers more goods—I did not mention about the cheque being torn up—he said nothing else than what I have repeated—the constable took him in charge.
Cross-examined. His duty was to pay it over immediately—Heath has not to my knowledge received money and not paid it over till next afternoon, it has been brought to me since this case—Heath is the junior hand—only one instance has come to my knowledge, and that at Green-wich Police-court, in which Heath left some money in one of the recesses against the shop fixtures and paid it over next morning, but we held the cheques at the same time—I had not given the prisoner notice to leave, I told him he was not suiting me, and if he did not attend to his duty better than he had been doing, I should have to make a change, no more was said—he gave me notice on the evening preceding this charge—he said he would stay on for 10 l. or for any sum providing I got rid of Heath—he has made complaints about Heath to me, and I have communicated them to Heath—it was Heath told me he saw the prisoner tear up the duplicate—the prisoner had been in my employment before, and when he came back he advised me to adopt this particular system of duplicates—after he left me the first time I did not write, and when he called offer him the situation a second time, I am sure of that—I don't think he came in after he left the first time, and said "If I had charge of this business again, I could make it pay well," I should say not—there was a lapse of about eighteen months between his leaving and being employed again—I might have written asking him to call, I believe it was to dress a window—I can swear I did not ask him if he was in earnest about what he said on the previous evening about making the business pay—I made an agreement with him—he came to me and said he was leaving, and I had to wait seven weeks till he could come into the situation—he came to the shop on this morning at his usual hour, nine; immediately he came in I told him to step upstairs, I did not say good morning to him—a detective was waiting outside—I asked him to explain to this gentleman about No. 39 cheque which was missing, he did not pull the money out of his pocket, he did not say "Here is the money, I intended to get another order and make the cheque larger," he said what I have stated previously—I asked him to explain about No. 39 which was missing, and he said "If you get my book you might find it there"—I knew that to be the 39 duplicate that Heath says the prisoner tore up in his presence—he volunteered that statement—I think I sent for the book—he did not look surprised when the book came and the duplicate was not there—I then said I should charge him with embezzling the money, and then he went down on his knees—he did not say he never intended to keep the money permanently—he was excited, the detective was in the room—he said nothing about "I admit having the money, but I never intended to embezzle it."
Re-examined. When I sent for the book in which he said I might find it, and No. 39 had vanished out of it, he made no observation whatever—there is very little in the book now, because part is given to the customer and part filed.
MARY STEVENS . I live at the Crown, Trafalgar Road, where I am
barmaid to Mrs. Lyons—on 21st October the prisoner came in the morning with some patterns for Mrs. Lyons to choose from, and he saw her then—I was there when he returned in the afternoon about three, and Mrs. Lyons gave him the order to bring eight yards of cashmere—he went and returned with eight yards, and she asked him to bring some trimming—I saw him give the cashmere to Mrs. Lyons and his bill, and I saw it receipted and he received 1 l. 9 s. 4 d. in my presence—I gave this bill to the police—there was a slight mistake in the price, and it was altered from 1 l. 10 s. 1 d. to 1 l. 19 s. 4 d.
Cross-examined. Mr. Newton's clerk came in with him when he was trying to effect this sale, so that the transaction appeared perfectly open and above board—the public-house is only two shops from Mr. Newton's—Mr. Newton does not come in, his people do—I have talked to Mrs. Lyons about this, and said that from what I could see and overheard at the police-court all the witnesses appeared to have a spite against this man.
Re-examined. I said that because they all seemed to have a spite against him the day I was at the police-court—that was from the evidence they gave as to what he did and said, and so on—nobody from the shop except the prisoner was present when he brought the cashmere over and received the 1 l. 9 s. 4 d., the other person was only there in the morning when the patterns were brought.
EVELINE HINES . I am Mr. Newton's head assistant on the fancy side—on 21st October he was away, and the prisoner had charge of the business—about ten minutes to twelve he came to me and said he was going to sell a cashmere dress to Mrs. Lyons, and I saw him take some cashmere away and go out of the shop—about four I saw him again, and he told me he had been unable to effect a sale with Mrs. Lyons, as we had not the velvet broche to match—at tea about five he repeated the same tale, and said he was very nearly cutting the length of cashmere off, that Mrs. Lyons would not have it as he had not got the velvet broche to match, and he added she was a very unforgiving woman.
Cross-examined. When he came back before dinner he said he was afraid he would not bo able to effect a sale as he had not got the velvet to match, and that he was taking the goods out to sell—it was done quite openly—I have had words with him on one occasion about matters of discipline—I was present on one or two occasions when he complained strongly and severely about Heath to Mr. Newton.
Re-examined. Just after the prisoner came back to Mr. Newton's after a customer had gone out to whom I could not sell some lace he said he would make it warm for me, and I said I was not afraid of him.
EMILY BROWN . I am in Mr. Newton's employment—I dissect the counterfoils given in with the cash and draw up the amounts, and see whether they correspond with the slips—dissecting means putting different goods to the credit of different departments—this is the book the prisoner had—on 21st October, between half-past three and four, the prisoner told me. he had given two cheques to a customer by mistake, and that I should find No. 39 missing in the morning—if goods have been sold it would have been his duty to hand in that counterfoil to the cashier, and it would have come into my hands next morning when I dissected—I heard the prisoner say to Miss Hines that he did not think he could sell the dress to Mrs. Lyons as there was only a yard and a
quarter of velveteen, and she wanted four yards—there is a youth Heath in the employment—I saw him that afternoon pick up some papers off the floor from behind the counter, and I saw him put them in the small pocket of his coat—I could not identify them at all.
Cross-examined. I was at the police-court twice, but only gave evidence once—I made a mistake before when I said that the prisoner instituted this system of duplicate cheques—I have not heard what the prosecutor said—I have heard him speaking to Mr. Newton about it, and also one of the others—before he came there were no duplicates, but we entered the amount on small cheques and balanced the money with the cheques—the prisoner would have a particular place to keep his book in the same as the rest—it would not be lying about the shop as a rule—I suppose he would put it anywhere he liked on his side—his name was written in it—I was not present when he charged Heath with stealing—when Heath picked up these pieces of paper he only said "I am going to have a look at these" as he put them in his pocket—it is an uncommon thing for bits of paper of that kind to be behind the counter—I never destroy a duplicate—if I make a mistake it is made void by the prisoner or Mr. Newton—the prisoner would have a right to make void a duplicate—he openly told me to make 39 void as he had given it by mistake to a customer—that was in Heath's presence—I don't know if he was listening—I don't think I told Mr. Newton at first I had seen Heath pick up the pieces of paper—I did the following morning—I have seen pieces of paper thrown down behind the counter before—I can't say how many pieces he picked up—this was about four; I can't fix the time—I have heard the prisoner talking to Mr. Newton about the discipline of the shop—he may have fined me; I think only once—I don't know if he told Mr. Newton he had fined me—he has spoken about Heath to Mr. Newton—I don't know what he has said—I think he has made complaints about him.
Re-examined. He said No. 39 would be missing in the morning.
FREDERICK HEATH . I am an assistant to Mr. Newton—between 3 and 4 on the afternoon of 21st Oct., I saw the prisoner taking some cashmere out of the shop—I saw him come back—I believe he had the goods then—he took out one parcel in white paper and brought a piece of white paper and the parcel, I believe, back with him; I don't know if there were patterns in it as well—when he came back I heard him say to Miss Brown No. 39 would be missing out of his book next morning, because he had given it to a customer by mistake—before that in the afternoon I was standing behind the counter and I saw the prisoner cut off a length of cashmere—shortly after he said that to Miss Brown I saw him come over to the other counter, open his book, take something out, tear it up, and throw it down behind the counter—I picked up the pieces and put them in my pocket; these are they—they were afterwards pasted together by Mr. Newton—I said something to Miss Brown about them.
Cross-examined. I don't think I said before the Magistrate anything about his giving as a reason for tearing up the duplicates that he had given it to a customer—I saw him cut the cashmere off—he would have a right to make void a duplicate—he has complained about me to Mr. Newton several times—one day it was about my breath smelling—I am not sure if he has complained of idleness—I believe he has of inattention to business—he has grumbled at me—he said I picked up a 6 d. behind
the counter—a constable was brought in and I was charged with and confessed in my employer's presence to taking the 6 d.—the prisoner complained to my employer of that—when he came in I could see he had not paid in any money—I believe he had his duplicate book with him when he came in—I can't remember whether I saw it or not afterwards that afternoon; I might have done—he might very often have been near where I was—there are often bits of torn-up paper lying about on the floor behind the counter—I made some remark to Miss Brown when I picked them up; I don't recollect what it was—I did not hear what she said to-day—I was not surprised at seeing paper on the floor, but I had a suspicion, so I picked it up—I won't be certain if Mr. Newton had come in before he tore them up; I will not swear he had not—I knew it was wrong for him to tear them up—I thought I had better wait till after business and give them to Mr. Newton when the shop was closed—the prisoner was standing at the counter when he tore it up—I knew it was missing and was surprised when I saw him take it out—I did not know what I was going to pick up; I thought that was it.
Re-examined. I had not stolen the 6 d., and the prisoner, after seeing Mr. Newton, accused me also of taking 2 s., and said I had better confess to one or the other or he would lock me up, and I then said I had taken it because of that, and that I thought it would worry my parents, but I had not stolen my master's 6 d.—my master knew it and kept me in his service.
By MR. KEITH FRITH. Before the prisoner spoke to me about the 6 d. he was closeted with Mr. Newton.
EMILY GOLDTHORPE . I am cashier to the prosecutor—no sum of 1 l. 9 s. 4 d. was paid in by the prisoner to me on the afternoon of 21st October for Mrs. Lyons's transaction—he brought me no duplicate numbered 39, or anything of the sort.
Cross-examined. I have been there about two months—previous to that I had no experience as a cashier—I have made several mistakes since I have been there, but never without having somebody to overlook them.
Re-examined. My duty is only to see if the duplicate corresponds with the money.
JOYCE ( Detective R). On Wednesday morning, 22nd October, about 9 o'clock, I was at Mr. Newton's shop by direction—the prisoner came upstairs; I asked him if he had sold any goods to Mrs. Lyons—he said "Yes"—I asked him if he had received any money, what he had done with it—he said "I received the money, and was going to keep it until Mrs. Lyons had some more goods, and then pay it in in one account"—he went on his knees and asked to be forgiven, as he would be rained—he was given into custody and taken to the station, where I told him he would be charged with embezzling 1 l. 9 s. 4 d., the property of his master—he said "I am guilty, I am guilty; I am a ruined man"—I searched him and found 1 l. 9 s. 7 d. in his purse—that was all he possessed.
Cross-examined. He did not say at the police-court that on being asked to explain he at once offered the money—he did not offer the money—his words were not "I am certainly guilty of receiving the money, but not of embezzling it"—I told the same story twice over at the police-court—I don't know if my deposition was lost—I had nothing to correct on the second occasion—the prisoner said on the first occasion when cross-examining me "I am guilty of having the money"—my version was that he only said "I am guilty, I am guilty; I am a ruined man."
The prisoner, being allowed to make his own statement to the Jury, said that having sold the cashmere he put the duplicate in his book and the money in his pocket; that as soon as he got to the shop he placed his book carelessly on the counter, and he had to serve some one, and forgot all about the money until he got home, when he laid it on his dressing-table, and that the next morning he said to his landlady "I left that money upstairs which I ought to have paid to Mr. Newton;" and that he quite believed the duplicate would be found in his book; he denied having stated "I am guilty" but asserted that what he said was "I am guilty of receiving the money, but I am not guilty of embezzlement; this will ruin me;" and he admitted having been previously convicted, but said that after that he had had a situation for two years and a splendid character.
Witness for the Defence.
ELLEN ORCHARD . I live at 13, Heston Street, Deptford—the prisoner has been lodging with me for two months up to his arrest, and always conducted himself honestly and respectably in every way—on the morning of his arrest, before he went out, he said to me that he had had a row with Mr. Newton and had to leave his place, that he had had some money the day before and had forgotten to pay it in, and was going to pay it in that morning—the next I heard was that he was in custody, and I then went to the police-court, and was bound never to be here to-day.
Witness in Rely.
DONALD ROBERTSON . I produce the certificate of the prisoner's conviction in July, 1874, at Newcastle-on-Tyne, of unlawfully obtaining money by false pretences, when he was sentenced to seven years' penal servitude—he had previously been twice convicted.
Cross-examined. He would get no remission the first year, but after that he could have deducted a quarter of the whole.
The prisoner received a good character since he had come from penal servitude from Mr. Wheatley, the Secretary of the St. Giles's Christian Mission.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18841117-70]
70. ROBERT HOLT , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. LLOYD and WILKINSON Prosecuted.
ALFRED HAYE . I am barman at the Centurion, Deptford Broadway—on 25th October, between 3 and 4 o'clock, the prisoner came in for twopennyworth of rum, giving me a florin, which I discovered was bad before I took it off the counter—I said "This is the piece you gave me; this is no good"—I bit it in two—he said "Let's look"—I laid one piece on the counter and turned round to my governor, and when I looked round the prisoner was gone—there was no one else in the compartment—I and my governor went and looked out of the door, but could not see anything of him—on the following week I was taken to Woolwich Police station, and picked the prisoner out from seven or eight other men—I had previously given a description of him.
Cross-examined. You had only to open the door—you were standing about a yard and a half from it—we were going to apprehend you.
ARTHUR HENRY COE . I am barman at the South-Eastern Distillery, New Cross Road—on 31st October I was in the bar about 11 a.m., when the prisoner came in for twopennyworth of rum, giving me this florin—I gave him a shilling, sixpence, and 4 d. bronze change—I had suspicions
of the florin when I did so, and afterwards showed it to the manager, and we sent King, the potman, after him—he had gone out directly he got the change—he was in the house about three minutes—he drank the rum.
Cross-examined. I had not seen you before that morning; I did then—I was not certain at the time I took it that it was bad, and went to ask the manager about it.
ALFRED KING . I was potman at the South-Eastern Distillery at Deptford on 31st October—I saw the prisoner served about 11 o'clock, as I was going through the bar—I saw no coin pass—soon after he left the barman gave me a bad coin, and said something in consequence of which I followed the prisoner into the Royal Albert, and went for a constable, as they were talking about the two-shilling-piece he had passed there—I found a constable at the corner of High Street, and coming back with him we met the prisoner on the road, and I gave him in charge—he was taken back to the Royal Albert, searched, and 1 1/2 d. was found on him.
Cross-examined. You were apprehended about 30 yards from the Royal Albert.
MARY EMPSON . My father keeps the Royal Albert, Deptford—at five minutes past 11 on the morning of 31st October I was serving at the bar, when the prisoner came in for twopennyworth of rum and gave this florin in payment, which I discovered to be bad—I said "It is a bad one; where did you get it?"—he said "I do not know," and went out when my back was turned—King came in two or three minutes afterwards, and then the constable—King brought the prisoner back, and he was taken away—I gave the florin to the constable.
Cross-examined. You said you were sorry for the mistake when I told you it was a bad one.
ALFRED ATTREE ( Policeman R 318). The Distillery is about three minutes' walk from the Royal Albert—a little after 11 on the morning of 31st October King spoke to me, in consequence of which I went in search of the prisoner, and found him about 20 yards from the Royal Albert, going towards Deptford—I said to him "You have been passing bad money"—he said "No, not me"—I said "You will have to go back with me"—I was about to take him back to the Distillery when Ernest Collard came up and spoke to me, and I then took the prisoner to the Royal Albert—the last witness gave me this bad florin which I have produced—I asked the prisoner how he came by the money—he said he had it given him for doing a job of work yesterday—he did not say where—I took him to the station and searched him, and found 1 1/2 d. on him.
Cross-examined. The Royal Albert is 20 yards from where you were apprehended.
ERNEST COLLARD . I am a baker, and live at Greenwich—on 31st October, about 11, I was in the Distillery having a glass of ale, when the prisoner came in for twopennyworth of rum—after he went out the barman found it was a bad coin, and I followed him with Kong—I saw him go into the Royal Albert, and detained him there till King fetched the constable—he was quiet when I detained him, and argued with the landlady about the coin, and then five fellows came in, and he said to them "This man charges me with passing bad money," and they said "You had better missle off; old man," then he went out, and I followed him till I met the constable and potman.
Cross-examined. I did not appear at the police-court—I was subpœnaed to come here.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . These coins are both bad, and from the same mould—I could not break a good coin in my teeth; I believe it has been done.
The prisoner in his defence stated that he was a hawker, and a stranger in London, and that he was guilty of offering one which he had had the day before.
GUILTY .— Twelve Months' Hard Labour.
Before Robert Malcolm Kerr, Esq.

72. WILLIAM JOSEPH LANGFORD (39) and AUGUSTA WILSON (35) , Stealing a watch, the property of Alice Lanson, to which
WILSON PLEADED GUILTY .
MR. WILKINSON Prosecuted.
ALICE LAUSON . I reside at 80, Ormside Street, Old Kent Road—the female prisoner is my daughter—the prisoner Langford had been living with her as her husband—on the 11th of this month, about 6 o'clock in the evening, my daughter was at my house—I had a gold watch then; it was hanging up on a hook over the mantelpiece—I missed it directly after she had gone—I had seen it safe at a quarter to 4 o'clock on that afternoon; this is it ( produced)—I value it at 10 l.
FREDERICK RICHARD PRESTCOTT . I am assistant at Rogers and Co.'s, pawnbrokers, 185, Walworth Road—I produce a watch pledged on the first occasion on 11th November, by the prisoner Langford, in the name of William Wilson, for 2 l.—he came again on 3rd November with the female prisoner; he said he had come to get a declaration on his watch, as he had lost his ticket—he said it was his own watch, a family relic—in consequence of that I made out a declaration, which he took away, and brought back and received a new ticket.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. You went outside and said you would ask your wife whether you should take the 2 l. which I offered or not, and you came back and said "I had better have the money."
GEORGE PURBROOK ( Police Sergeant R). I arrested the prisoners—Wilson said in Langford's presence "I stole the watch, Langford was waiting at the top of the street; he pawned it the same evening in the Walworth Road"—Langford said "That is quite right."
ALFRED MARSDEN ( Policeman R 41). I took Langford in charge—he said "She stole the watch and gave it to me, and I pawned it for 2 l."—he gave me the ticket, which I produce.
The Prisoner's Statement before the Magistrate. "She handed me the watch and I pawned it; she had the money and bought herself some clothes with it. She told me her mother had given her the watch to pawn to raise the wind so as she could get some clothes and look after a respectable situation."
Witness for Langford.
AUGUSTA WILSON ( The Prisoner). I have been living with the prisoner—I didn't say anything to him about the watch before I got it; when I got it I took it to him at the top of the street and gave it to him—I told
him mother had lent it to me, and would he pawn it, as I wanted to get some better clothes to get a situation; he did pawn it, and I stood outside—I lost the ticket.
LANGFORD — NOT GUILTY . WILSON — Two Months' Hard Labour.

[trialAccount t18841117-73]
73. FREDERICK YOUNG (24) and CHARLES RANDALL (50) were indicted for unlawfully obtaining goods by false pretences from various persons, and also for conspiring with others not in custody to defraud.
MESSRS. POLAND and MONTAGU WILLIAMS Prosecuted.
FRANCIS HENRY TURNER . I am managing clerk to Messrs. Coulthurst and Harding, of the Phoenix Colour Works, Temple Gate, Bristol—in January last I received this order from Frederick Young. ( This was an order from F. Young, wholesale and retail oil and colour Italian warehouse, Melbourne Grove, Champion Rood, Battersea, for paints and other articles.) We supplied these goods—I next received an order for 5 cwt. of white lead, which I also supplied on the 2nd February—I afterwards received on the 8th February this order for varnishes—I sent those goods—I then received an order for a barrel of petroleum and a barrel of gasoline, signed "A. Young, pro F. Young"—I did not send that—the goods altogether that I sent amounted to 18 l.—I believed this F. Young was carrying on a genuine business.
Cross-examined by Young. I received the first order through the post-office—I had a traveller named Spurgeon—I did not receive the first order through him—I didn't see the goods delivered, but I have the carrier's signature for the three parcels.
JAMES DENNIS . I am a ropemaker, of 39, Wandsworth Road—I supplied goods to Young, of Champion Hill, upon an order brought by a traveller—I handed that order to the solicitor—I supplied clothes-lines and box-cord to Young, amounting to 7 l. 8 s. 6 a.—the terms were cash on delivery—I have not received it—I applied for payment, and received this letter, signed F. Young, stating that he would call on the earliest opportunity to settle—I received this receipt, dated 31st January, for the goods, signed "A. Young."
Cross-examined by Young. My traveller is not here—you came to my shop on the Tuesday following, and asked for a longer time—I said I shouldn't give it.
FREDERICK HALL . I am carman to Mr. Dennis—I delivered the goods to Young, of Champion Hill, and received this receipt—Young signed it in my presence.
ALFRED WILLIAM HODSON . I represent Bowley and Sons, oil merchants, of Wellington Works, Battersea—on 18th January, and on 23rd January and 4th February, we supplied goods to F. Young, of Melbourne Grove, Champion Hill, amounting to 2 l. 17 s. 9 d., 5 l. 8 s., and 2 l. 3 s. respectively—I produce the carman's receipts for the delivery of the goods—I have applied for the money several times, but I could not get it—I believed the business to be a bona fide one—we sued for the amount, and judgment went by default.
Cross-examined by Young. The order was taken through our traveller—we received no references, but we have received nine or ten inquiries in consequence of you making use of our name without authority as a reference.
JOSEPH DUNK . I am a carman to Messrs. Bowley—I delivered the goods in question on three occasions to Young, of Champion Hill, and I produce the receipts in my book for the delivery.
Cross-examined by Young. I did not see the book signed; it was signed in a back room.
FRANK NEEDHAM . I am traveller to W.G. Clark and Sons, biscuit manufacturers, of Thompson Street, Limehouse—in February last I received this order from F. Young, of Melbourne Grove, for 2 cwt. of buffalo dog biscuits—I forwarded them to the amount of 1 l. 6 s., and received this receipt for the delivery, signed "E. Young"—on 21st February a gentleman called, who represented himself to be Mr. Young, not either of the prisoners—he gave me a verbal order for 5 cwt. of biscuits, amounting to 3 l. 5 s., and paid the first order of 1 l. 6 s.—he signed this receipt "Arthur Young," and took away the 5 cwt. with him—that order was not paid for—on the 12th March I received a further order for 5 cwt. of biscuits, signed "F. Young"—I did not execute that order—on 17th March I received this card. ( This was headed "Elm Park Stores, J. Smart, Proprietor Agent, &c. "Being just about to open business I have had your dog biscuit strongly recommended. Please forward 2 cwt. as sample; also showcards. Yours truly, J. Smart.")—I wrote to Mr. Smart for reference, and he gave Mr. Young as a reference—I did not send the goods—we thought Young's reference insufficient.
THOMAS IMESON . I live at 145, Dulwich Road, and am a commission agent and traveller—I sell on commission for Garnham Brothers, of 163, High Street, Peckham—on 22nd February I called at Melbourne Grove, Champion Hill, at a shop with the name of F. Young over it, it appeared to be an oil shop—I saw the prisoner Young there and asked him to give me an order—he said he could do with five hundredweight of biscuits and he would pay cash for them—he gave me this billhead with the name of F. Young on it; the goods were sent by Mr. Garnham—on 10th March I called and saw Young, and he gave me a further order for five hundredweight of biscuits and other goods amounting to 8 l. 8 s. 9 d., I believe they were delivered—I told the prisoner that unless he paid the first order the second would not be delivered, and he said he would call round and pay Mr. Garnham—I afterwards called for payment, I did not see the prisoner again, I saw a younger man—I got no money.
Cross-examined by Young. I saw you when I first called, I am sure of that—the shop was open and it looked as if it had plenty of stock in it.
EDWARD CHARLES GARNAHAM . I am a corn-chandler, of 165, High Street, Peckham—on 26th January I received these orders from Imeson—I supplied five hundredweight of biscuits at 12 s. per hundredweight—they were paid for on 11th March—on 10th March Imeson brought me a second order—on the 11th Young called and said he had given an order to our traveller for another lot of goods which he wanted executed as soon as possible—I sent them—I was not paid for those—I called several times, I saw a man who represented himself as Young's brother—I saw F. Young once, and I said "I have called for your account"—he said "No, not me"—the goods were supplied in the ordinary way of trade—
I believed the firm was a genuine one from the appearance of the place—on 27th March Randall called on me and asked the price of dogs' biscuits—I told him 12 s. per hundredweight, and he said "Send me five hundredweight"—he gave me this card headed "Elm Park Stores, 74, Elm Park, Brixton, Mr. J. Smart, proprietor"—I said "We don't know these people, do they pay cash?"—he said "Yes, and if there is no one there of course you will leave them and the cash will be forwarded on"—I said "No, if they want credit they must give satisfactory references," and I heard nothing more from them.
Cross-examined by Young. When you paid me for the first lot of goods I did not give you terms for the others, I said nothing about terms—you told me that my traveller had agreed to give you a month's credit, and I said "Well then I will not call for a month."
Cross-examined by Randall. You told me that Mr. Smart had sent you to ask for the price of the biscuits, and I told you that cash for the first order and after that On terms; I said "Unless we hear from you we shall not excuse it"—I did not say "Shall I send 5 cwt. on approbation?"
CHARLES KING I am carman to Mr. Garnham—I delivered 5 cwt. of biscuits at Melbourne Grove.
Cross-examined by Young. The goods were received by A. Young, not by you, I think.
WILLIAM BYFORD . I am an oilman, of Maxtead Road, Peckham—in March last Young called and ordered some goods, and offered me 2 cwt. of biscuits, which I agreed to take at 10 s. 6 d. per cwt.—I had been dealing with Mr. Garnham, and that was 1 s. per cwt. less than Mr. Garnham—Young called on me again and asked me if I wanted to buy more goods, I refused—Randall came to my place in February for oil—I knew from a man I bought varnishes of that he was coming—Randall said the oil was for Mr. Young, of Melbourne Grove.
Cross-examined by Randall. You paid for the oil.
EDWARD MANNING . I am traveller for Messrs. Anderson, of 10, London Street, Fenchurch Street, felt manufacturers—on 14th March I supplied goods to Young, of Champion Hill; this is the delivery note ( produced) for twelve rolls, signed "A. Young"—the amount was about 10 l.—on 25th March I again delivered goods, this is the delivery note signed "M. Young"—I believed the business to be a genuine one, and on that I supplied the goods—on 19th March I received this letter signed "J. Smart," stating that he was recommended to us by Young, of Champion Hill and ordering ten rolls of roping, I did not supply it—I went to Elm Park Stores, and found no business going on there; I referred to Young, who said he had known him for some years, and had done a little business with him, giving him credit for 2 l. or 3 l., and he had always paid him; this card was in the letter.
Cross-examined by Young. I don't think anything was said about terms. I said I could not give long discount—Smart did not refer to you, I merely asked him what he knew of you.
WILLIAM LADSLEY . I am carman to John Smothers and Sons, of Lower East Smithfield—I delivered the goods from Messrs. Anderson, and to the best of my belief saw the prisoner Young sign the name of A. Young.
JAMES MORGAN . I am carman to Edwin Ellis, of Honey Lane,
Bermondsey—I delivered the second parcel of goods from Messrs. Anderson to Young—the delivery note was signed by a female.
MARIA LOUISA WALTERS . I am the wife of George Walters, of Church Street, Camberwell, a pianoforte manufacturer, he is ill—on 19th March our traveller Beard brought the prisoner Young and another man, he introduced them to me as the two Youngs, they selected a black and gold piano at the price of thirty-six guineas—they gave their address at Melbourne Grove, Champion Hill; they said they kept an oil shop—I agreed to let the piano out on the hire system, and this document ( produced) was signed by the prisoner Young and the other person who represented to be his brother, as F. Young, 3, Dulwich Terrace, Champion Hill, and Arthur Young, name address—the piano was sent—the prisoner signed "Frederick Young" and the other one "Arthur Young"—we never got our piano back nor yet any money for it—my husband went to that address—he is at home ill—he has not been able to come out since last week—I was at the police-court when he was examined—I saw him sworn and heard him give his evidence, and saw him sign his deposition.
Cross-examined by Young. It was arranged that Mr. Walters should call before we sent the piano, but he did not call; he sent the piano.
( The deposition of George Walters was here put in and read, stating that he sent the piano by Smythe, the carman; that he afterwards called, but found the shop closed.)
HENRY SMYTHE . I am carman to Mr. Walters—on 19th March I delivered a piano at Champion Hill to the prisoner Young—I gave this agreement to him, and he signed it, and his brother signed it.
Cross-examined by Young. I swear positively you signed "Frederick Young."
ALFRED KNIGHT . I live at 90, Villiers Street, Walworth, and am a grocer—I bought a piano of Young after the March quarter—I saw it at his shop at Champion Hill—the name of Young was over the shop—I gave him 12 l. for it—he showed me a piece of paper, and said the brokers had sued for rent—I understood the piano was not on hire from what he said to me—it was taken to my premises, and has been claimed by Mr. Walters—I have made some arrangement with him.
Cross-examined by Young. You called at my place on 26th March—I showed you that the broker had distrained on the piano for rent—I don't recollect you first asking me to advance you 12 l.; you might have done so—I saw a man in possession there—I brought the 12 l. with me, and saw the piano—you paid me the 12 l., and I sent you the receipt in the morning.
JAMES FOX . I am clerk to Henry R. Condy, of Battersea, a white lead manufacturer—we had a travelling man named Toby—we supplied through him two quarterns of white lead, value 4 l. 13 s., to Frederick Young, of Champion Hill, and we received this receipt, signed "A. Young:" I received this postcard, signed "Frederick Young," requesting the lead without fail on Monday—on 7th April I received this other order for five cwt. of lead, value 4 l.—on the 10th a man came and fetched it, and gave this receipt, signed "A. Young."
Cross-examined by Young. We received this order through Toby, out traveller—he is not here; we are in search of him—this is not the only order of the kind that we received through him.
WILLIAM EASTER . I am carman to Mr. Condy—I delivered these goods at 5, Melbourne Grove.
Cross-examined by Young. I believe A. Young signed for them—I cannot remember you—I only saw you for two minutes.
GEORGE ALDRIDGE . I am manager to Howard and Co., drysalters, 143, New Kent Road—about four months ago we supplied goods to Frederick Young, of Melbourne Grove, to the amount of 3 l. 6 s. 6 d.—Mr. Welsford is our traveller—our terms are cash on delivery—cash not being paid, on the following Saturday I called at the oil shop in Melbourne Grove—I saw some one; neither of the prisoners—that person made an appointment for the following week—I then saw the prisoner Young—I told him I came after the account, 3 l. 6 s. 6 d.—he said Mr. Young was not in; he would be in next Saturday—I called then and saw the other person—he said Young was not in—I used some strong language, and the prisoner Young then came forward—he said "I am Mr. Young, what have you got to say?"—I said "You might have told me that in the first instance," and I believe I called him a swindler—he did not pay me—I left, and never got any money—while this transaction was going on Randall called at our place, gave me this card, ( As before), and ordered some goods amounting to 10 l. or 12 l.—I asked for a reference, and he gave Mr. Young, of Champion Hill—I did not go there; I told Eandall that Young's reference was hardly good enough, and we did not deliver the goods—while I was at Champion Hill I saw Randall there—he was taking goods away in a small trap—I saw him there four or five times; he was taking away goods more than once.
Cross-examined by Randall. You told me you were manager to Mr. Smart—you did not say anything about terms; you simply gave me the order—I saw you taking away several descriptions of goods; several cwt. of soap for one thing, and a bag of dog biscuits—the soap was not wrapped up—I saw you loading other goods on other occasions, but I am not able to specify what.
Cross-examined by Young. Your order was given through our traveller—he is not here—I did not see the goods delivered.
SAMUEL QUINT . I live at 116, Manor Road, Brockley—I am owner of the shop, 3, Doveridge Terrace, Melbourne Grove, Champion Hill—I let that shop to the prisoner Young from 14th January—he said that he and his brother wished to go into business, that they had got very little money to start with, something about 30 l.—I said I would do all I possibly could to assist them—I let them the shop with fixtures and stock—he signed this agreement "Frederick Young," at a rent of 50 l. a year—he gave me 5 l. in part payment and a promissory note for 25 l. signed by the prisoner and Albert Stanley—Stanley came to my place as a reference, and gave his address as 408, Albert Road, Battersea Park Road—he said he knew both the Youngs and their parents, and he thought they were very honest, straightforward young chaps —he called with one of the Youngs—the quarter's rent became due in March—I got 10 l. the day after by putting in the brokers, from what the police told me—I went and found they had gone and the door left open—they took away some of the fixtures worth 4 l. or 5 l., and the stock was all gone.
Cross-examined by Young. You took possession of the place about the middle of January—I put in a distress the day after the rent was due—I
believe it was your brother who called with Stanley—you and your brother were at the place together—I could not swear who took the key—I did not see the agreement signed—the solicitor saw you sign it—I did not see the promissory note signed—I saw you when you made the agreement.
ALFRED DAVIS . I am agent to Mr. Fletcher, who is landlord of 5, Honor Oak Terrace, Forest Hill—I let those premises to a man who gave the name of James Ward under this agreement ( produced) at a rent of 26 l. a year—to the best of my belief the prisoner Young is the person—he paid a deposit of 1 l.—I did not get any rent—I distrained—I received this understanding in the name of Frederick Wood—I forgave him the rent and he took the goods.
Cross-examined by Young. It is a private house—I did not see the document signed—I kept the 1 l. deposit when you gave up possession—3 l. 5 s. is owing.
SARAH BIGGS . I live, at 76, Abingdon Road, Kensington—I am the owner of 221, Shakespeare Road—on 17th June I let that house to the prisoner Rand all under this agreement ( produced) at 28 l. a year—it is a private house—I did not see Randall sign the agreement, the agent saw him—he referred me to Mr. Strange, who keeps a little oilshop at 113, Station Road, Forest Gate—I saw Randall write this: "Charles Randall, 7, Santley Street, Acre Lane, Brixton. Mr. Strange, 113, Station Road, Forest Gate"—he had the premises up to the time he was taken into custody—I was not able to get any rent—he gave up the keys to a young woman who called him father—he said he took the house for himself and wife.
Cross-examined by Randall. You owe me a quarter's rent and I wish you would pay me—I did not apply for any rent till the 29th, and there was no one there.
WILLIAM PREDDLE . I live at 165, Elm Park, Brixton Rise—my cousin, Mr. Preddle, is landlord of 74, Elm Park—in February last an old man came and took that house—he is not here—Randall afterwards came with him—the old man introduced him as his manager who he would put into the shop till his younger son could take it; that his son was employed by Mr. Young, of Champion Hill, and in the mean time Randall would take possession of the shop and manage it for him—the reference given by Smart was Mr. Young, of Melbourne Grove, and Mr. Randall, S, Oscar Terrace, Beckton Road—I communicated with those addresses and got these replies. ( The one from Young stated that he had known Smart some years, that the son had been manager of his shop for some time and he had no doubt he would be a desirable tenant and able to pay the rent. The reply from Randall stated that Smart had been a tenant of his for some years, that he had always found him prompt in his payments, and that he would no doubt be a desirable and responsible tenant.) Upon receiving those references I agreed to let the shop, and Smart signed this agreement in Randall's presence—the place was never opened for business—I called the day after the rent was due and found the place shut up and the man gone—I had seen Randall there in the mean time, and occasionally I saw the old man there—I do not know Young—I saw a young person who was represented by the old man to be his son, but I have since understood him to be Young's brother.
ROBERT POLE . I live at 3, Barking Road, Plaistow—I am proprietor
of 8, Yaelm Terrace, Beckton Road—on 1st November I let that shop to a man named Stanley on this agreement at a rental of 3.5 l.—Stanley took possession of the premises—the place was opened in a week, but he never opened them for business—I received references to F. Fry and F. Williams—I saw both the prisoners at the premises—I knew Randall by the name of Stanley; at least I never could get at his name—when I inquired his name he said "Oh, call me anything you like, Jones, Robinson, or anything you like"—he said he was manager there for Stanley—I saw people come and deliver goods there, and they went away again almost directly in carpet bags, boxes, carts, &c., at different times—I saw Eandall there in company with Stanley many times—he and his daughter lived in the house and two women and a lodger—I got no rent or a halfpenny for the fixtures—I put in a broker for the rent—there were some goods on the premises which were claimed by a man named Shaw, who took them away—he represented himself as a lodger—Randall claimed the rest of the goods—I offered to forgive him the rent if he would go; he refused to go—the goods were impounded—subsequently Randall moved them away—very nearly all my fixtures were cleared out, about 10 l. worth—I never got a sixpence.
Cross-examined by Randall. Goods were sent there after the first time I saw you—I don't know what they were—the previous tenant owed rent and left me the fixtures to dispose of; they were my fixtures.
WILLIAM EAST . I live at Mary Street, Barking Road—I am agent to the landlord of 8, Oscar Terrace, Beckton Road—on 28th January last I let those premises to a Mr. Shaw at a weekly rental of 6 s.—on 4th Feb. I received the rent—I continued to receive it—Shaw's young man occupied the premises—he left in September and I think went to live at Brixton—I think I have seen Eandall about the Beckton Road, but had no business with him.
STANLEY ROGERS ( Policeman P 129). In February, March, April, and May I was taking care of 2, Melbourne Grove, Doverage Terrace, adjoining the Railway Oil Stores—I had occasion to notice what was going on there, and kept observation on the premises—I have seen the two prisoners there, and also a brother of Frederick Young named Arthur, and an elderly man; I did not know his name—there was very little business in the shop—I saw goods arrive from time to time, and as they were brought by the vans they were taken direct to a stable at the rear of the premises, put into a cart with the name of Hardy on it, and taken away—I should say I have seen fifteen loads taken away, principally in the night time—one load of dog biscuits in sacks were taken away the same day they came—I have seen the prisoner Young in the house and his brothers, and also Eandall; he was always there—on 12th May at six in the morning some fixtures and furniture were taken away by Randall, assisted by Samuel Hardy—I traced those things to 5, Honor Oak Terrace, Forest Hill Road—I afterwards went to 221, Shakespeare Road, and there saw Randall and Mrs. Young—there is a warrant out against Arthur Young; I have tried to find him and cannot—I don't know what has become of him.
Cross-examined by Randall. I saw you packing the cart with goods, assisting to load—you went with the van on two occasions—I had some conversation with you on the morning of 12th May, when the van was
loaded with the fixtures—you went away with the van—you told me you were going to make a clean breast of it.
Cross-examined by Young. I never saw you when they were moving the fixtures—my wife sometimes dealt with you—I have never seen you and your brother out delivering goods in the neighbourhood; only when you were going away with a van load of goods—you had a barrow there—I never saw you delivering goods in that—I have seen the shop regularly opened and seen plenty of stock there and upstairs also, but it has not been there very long—I have not said that I never saw a shop look so well—I have seen barrels there—I don't know whether they were full or empty.
Re-examined. I have seen Young there on occasions when goods were removed—he has helped to take them away.
DANIEL MORGAN ( Police Inspector X). I know the prisoner Young by the name of Thomas Shaw and Thomas Hawes—I know his father; he has gone by 100 names, but I believe his correct name is James Shaw—I know Randall; Charles Thomas Randall is his correct name—in February, 1876, he was living at 11, Goose Green, Dulwich, with Young and his father, old Shaw, and the other brother who is now wanted; they were then in the name of Shaw and Hawes, all living there together—Randall married Young's elder sister; I have the marriage certificate—I cannot find old Shaw or the brother; they have absconded.
Cross-examined by Young. I first knew you swindling with a man named Hall—you were at 37, Charles Street, Hatton Garden, in February, 1876, when I called there in respect of certain frauds when Frank Hall was arrested—I swear it was you—I did not take you into custody then, because you were only 17, and I thought your father was the principal person; he was arrested—I have not found you anywhere since.
CHARLES VINEY ( Police Sergeant P). I received Young in custody on this charge—I read the warrant to him—he said "Oh, that is only a debt"—it was for Mr. Garnham's dog-biscuits—I said "Well, it may be, if it stood alone; there is the piano at Mr. Walter's, at Camberwell, a sewing machine in the Borough, and many others"—he made no reply to that—I said "There is the shop in Oglander Road, last year"—he said "I never was in Oglander Road"—I said "You were, and you had goods there, and went away, and took the fixtures, and never paid any rent"—he said "I never was there"—I had seen him there on several occasions last year, and two or three years ago in a shop in Victoria Road, Peckham, which was never opened—after Young's arrest I went to 221, Shakespeare Road, and there saw Randall—I told him I was a police officer, and had a warrant for his arrest for being concerned with Young in fraud at Champion Hill—he said "What name have you got on the warrant?"—I said "Stanley"—he said "My name is not Stanley; you have made a mistake"—I said "Well, there is very little in names; you will have to go with me; there is the shop at Elm Park, Brixton, where you; were"—his daughter said, in his presence, "I had to do with all at Elm Park; I took in all that came there; my father knew nothing about it"—he said "I was only acting as manager there"—Young was by the name of Shaw at Victoria Road, and Sadler at Oglander Road.
Cross-examined by Young. The name of Wright was over the door at Oglander Road; he went away prior to your taking it—I saw you in the shop—your father no doubt took the shop, and you went there
as manager—you took the shop in Victoria Road of a man named Thornton—I swear I saw you there.
Young in his defence stated that he and his brother took the shops jointly; that they quarrelled, and his brother, unknown to him, cleared everything out; that he had no intention to defraud any one, and the cases against him were properly cases for the County Court, and not for a criminal one; Randall alleged that he only acted as a servant occasionally employed, and that there was nothing to show that he was connected with any conspiracy, or with the obtaining of any goods.
GUILTY .— Eighteen Months' Hard Labour each.
[trialAccount t18841117-74]
74. MARY ROONEY (69) , Feloniously forging and uttering a receipt for 5 l., with intent to defraud.
MR. MONTAGU WILLIAMS Prosecuted.
MARY ANN FARRELL . I am a widow, of 149, Snows Fields—the prisoner lives at 86, Paradise Street, Rotherhithe—about 12 months ago I paid 20 l. into the post-office at Blackman Street, and got this book ( produced)—I had drawn out all but 5 l., and last February I went to draw that out, the prisoner was with me, and for some reason I could not get the money that day—the prisoner went home with me—she is in the habit of running in and out of my place, and she said "Lend me that book to show to my lodger"—that was Mrs. Keeling—two days afterwards the prisoner said to me "I have got the money out"—I did not believe it, but she noticed it in her hand—I had not given her authority to take it out, or to sign my name, or to produce my book—some long time afterwards the prisoner said "There is your book"—the signature in it is not in my writing; I can't write—I went and spoke to the postmaster, and then told the prisoner I found she had drawn the money out—she told me to summons her—I said "No, I will go where I put my money"—she then said "You won't get your money there"—when I asked her how she got it she said "They knew me, I live near."
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. Mrs. Keeling is a stranger to me—I don't know that she owed you 12 l. for rent—when I went with you to the post-office you asked a lady there to sign the order for me, but that was torn up.
FRANCES KEELING . I am the wife of Thomas Keeling—I never had a talk with Mrs. Farrell about lending her 5 l.
ANNIE CORBY . My husband is a cheesemonger—I was in Mrs. Farrell's shop when the prisoner came in and asked her how she got on; she said "Very badly indeed; I could do very well if I had that 5 l. which you have so fraudulently taken from the post-office"—the prisoner said "Don't go to them or I shall not pay you; if you go to the post-office they will prosecute me, and then you will not be paid by anybody, I will not give you a farthing"—on 23rd August she came again while I was there, and said "Mrs. Farrell, I have got good news for you, I am going to get a loan and pay you the money; live you been to the post-office?" she said "No, I have been very ill, but I shall go"—she said "Don't; if you go they will prosecute me, and then I shall not pay you a farthing"—she showed me her book, and I expected to hear that her money had been drawn out; up to that time she was not quite sure.
ALEXANDER HORNE . I am a carpenter, of 52, Long Lane, Borough—the prisoner came to me and asked me to fill up this form to the post-master and sign it for Mrs. Farrell for 5 l., as Mrs. Farrell was ill and
unable to come—a few days afterwards she brought me this other form to the postmaster, "Pay Mary Ann Farrell 5 l."—I did not sign "Mary Ann Farrell," but I witnessed the mark—I never saw Mrs. Farrell from beginning to end—I went with the prisoner to the post-office and saw her produce the order and get the money, and she gave me 2 d. for a glass of ale.
EMMA WATKINS . My husband is a boot and shoe maker, of 38, Tooley Street—the prisoner rented two rooms in our house, and early this year she owed me 2 l. 8 s. for rent—I asked her for it, and she said "I am going to the post-office to get some money"—she went next door to Mr. Thorne and she and I and Mr. Thorne went to the post-office together—I saw Mr. Thorne write something on a paper and the prisoner took up a pen and put a cross to it, handed it to the postmaster, and got the money.
EMILY GORDON . I am a clerk in the General Post-office—on 14th February I received this notice to withdraw and sent this order to pay, addressed, "Mary Ann Ford, 29, College Street, Tooley Street," by post.
HARRIET TRIBE . I am a clerk at the District Post-office, Blackman Street, Borough—this warrant to pay was brought to me, the address is in my writing—it is always a practice to put the cross in my presence—I paid the money.
GEORGE HARVEY ( Police Sergeant M). I charged the prisoner on 6th October—she made no reply at the time—2 l. 17 s. 7 1/4 d. was found on her—she then said "Oh, it is by the will of God I am here."
The prisoner in, her statement before the Magistrate and in her defence said that Mrs. Farrell had been robbing her for six months, and this does done through spite.
MARY ANN FARRELL ( Re-examined). That is not true—I asked her to give me some of the money to pay the rent, she has never paid me a farthing—I worked for her for six months after I got it back, and never had a farthing—I had a child to keep and I am destitute, I have nothing left but a bedstead—the Magistrate gave me money to get my child provided for—the prisoner sent her son to represent me as dead when she got a loan, she has let me starve.
GUILTY .*— Fifteen Months' Hard Labour.
[trialAccount t18841117-75]

78. WILLIAM JONES (20) , Robbery with violence on John Crawley, and stealing a watch and chain, his property.
MR. PELLEW Prosecuted; MR. BLACKWELL Defended.
JOHN CRAWLEY . I am a labourer, of 6, Earl's Place, Queen Elizabeth Street—I am a cripple—on 25th October, at 8 p.m., I was in the Wallace public-house, Borough—I felt unwell, went out, and saw the prisoner there—I had seen him before—I went in—I asked him to direct me—
we went through an archway near the bridge—he said "Here you are all right," and he and a man not in custody sprang upon me, laid me down, and nearly choked me—they unbuttoned my coat—the other man held me by my throat, and the prisoner took my watch—they went towards the Borough Market, and I got up and told a constable—I saw the prisoner next day by the same public-house, and went and told a constable, and when I returned he was gone—I saw him again an hour after at a whelk-stall, and when I made a noise with my crutch he walked into a public-house—a policeman fetched him out, and I charged him with stealing my watch—he said I had made a mistake, but I am sure he is the man—he was remanded for a week, during which I came home on a Thursday, and found my watch there—it is worth 12 s.
Cross-examined. The policeman said I was under the influence of drink, because I stumbled over a carpet—my carman and I had had one or two pints—I never told any one that I thought the prisoner was the man, though I saw a fellow very much like him on the same night, selling boxes in the London Bridge Tavern, and I made a report at the police-station—he said "Don't you know me?"—I said "You look like the man I am looking after"—I did not say "I have just lost my watch and chain, and I think you have it"—he did not say "Do you think I have got it?" nor did I say "I don't know, but you are very much like the party; had I not seen you with those goods you are selling I should have given you in charge." ( A man named Brindsley was here brought into Court), that man was with him; they were selling boxes together—I have not said that I would not appear if the prisoner's friends gave me 10 s.; but two fellow, strangers to me, came up, and said "If we give you 30 s., and send your watch back, will you take no notice? we know where it is"—I said "Send the watch back first"—I did not send word to the prisoner's mother, I did not know where she lived, but she came crying to the police-court on the 27th, and offered me money.
BENJAMIN POLLEN ( Policeman). I was on duty on London Bridge on 26th Oct., and at 9.45 p.m. Crawley spoke to me, he was called away by another man, he went to the London Bridge Tavern and returned, we then went to a public-house, and the prisoner was there drinking with other men, and before Crawley gave him in custody some of the men said, "That is not him," he was afterwards pointed out to me and I took him in custody—he said, "I am innocent, I can prove where I was; I was at the music hall till twelve;" but before the Magistrate he said, "I did not say so, I said that I was at the Drummond public-house, Snowsfields, from eight till twelve."
Cross-examined. I made no note of the conversation—I did not think it necessary.
Re-examined. I received the watch from Crawley on the 30th, at a little before nine o'clock.
JOHN PENNY ( Policeman). On 25th October, at 7.30 p.m., I was on duty under the dry Arch of London Bridge—I saw the prisoner and Crawley—as they passed the prisoner said, "It is all right, governor"—I made no reply and they passed on; Crawley came back in ten minutes and complained to me, I referred him to the station—on Monday I went to the station and picked out the prisoner from nine other men—I had seen him before, and have no doubt he is the man—Crawley was the worse for drink.
WILLIAM BESLEY ( Policeman M 98). On 25th Ootober I was on duty at London Bridge Tavern about 7.15 p.m., and saw the prisoner outside—about a quarter of an hour after that Crawley came and complained to me—I am sure the prisoner is the man.
EDWARD CARPENTER . I am landlord of Drummond's public-house, Snow Street—on 3rd November I saw the prisoner at the police-court—I had never seen him before—if he had come to my house on Saturday, October 25th, I must have seen him, and should have known him.
Cross-examined. There were between forty and fifty people in my house between eight and twelve on October 25th, popping in and out—a young man was lying dead there, and there was an entertainment there to raise money for him—I have not told Mrs. Stanton that I could not be sure whether he was there or not—I said that I did not see the prisoner, but that people may have popped in and out.
Re-examined. I might not have noticed people who popped in and out, but if anybody had stopped there from eight till twelve I must have noticed them.
GUILTY of the robbery only.
He then PLEADED GUILTY † to a conviction at Southwark Police-court, in February, 1884.— Twelve Months' Hard Labour.
[trialAccount t18841117-79]
79. GEORGE SMITH (22) , Robbery with violence on Ellen Carter, and stealing a chain, a locket, and a trinket, her property.
MR. PELLEW Prosecuted.
ELLEN CARTER . My husband keeps a coffee-house at 40, New Kent Road—on 5th Ootober, about 9.20, I was opposite St. George's Church in the Borough, waiting for an omnibus, and the prisoner rushed upon me, tore my mantle down, and got my gold chain, which was under it, and a gold pencil case, locket, and pen on it—he had a cap with a peak to it—he rushed up a court towards the back of the Mint—I went to the station and made a complaint—I picked him out at the station among eight others on 16th October; he asked me if I was sore of him, I said "Yes, I believe you are the man"—I can now swear he is the man—the articles were worth about 11 l.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. You were the only man with a cap; all the others had hats.
THOMAS PICKLES ( Police Sergeant M). On 16th October, about eight p.m., I saw the prisoner at the corner of Mint Street, and said "I shall take you to the station for identification"—he said "What for?"—I said "I will tell you when we get to the station"—when the charge was read he said nothing—he was placed with eight others, and Mrs. Carter pointed to him directly, and said "I believe that is the man"—he said "I am innocent"—he said to Mrs. Carter "I believe you said just now that you believed I was the man?"—she said "Yes, I do believe so"—he gave his address at Godding's lodging-house, Red Cross Street, which was correct—he said next day before the Magistrate that he was in bed at nine o'clock on the night of the robbery.
Cross-examined. I was talking to you about eight p.m. on the Monday—I did not arrest you then because I had not then seen the prosecutrix.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in bed at nine o'clock; she said at first that
it was done at nine o'clock, and now she says twenty minutes past nine. I am innocent.
GUILTY of robbery only.
He then PLEADED GUILTY ** to a conviction of housebreaking in 1881.— Five Years' Penal Servitude.
[trialAccount t18841117-80]
80. EMILY BUDD (42) , Unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. GOODRICH Prosecuted.
WILLIAM HUBBARD . I keep the Surrey Arms public-house, Wilcox Road, Lambeth—on 18th October, about half-past eleven a.m., I was serving, when the prisoner came in for a pint of four ale, and tendered a half-crown—I gave her 2 s. 4 d. change, and then tested the half-crown in my teeth, which sank into it—I told her it was bad, and asked where she got it—she said at a butcher's on the terrace—I said "What butcher's?"—she said "From Mr. Reed, or one of the other butchers"—she then gave me a good shilling, and gave me back my change, and I returned her the half-crown—I was quite sure it was bad, and quite sure she was the woman—I did nothing besides biting it; two or three of my customers handled it, and some of them followed her—she would not let them damage it in any way because she said she wanted to take it back.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. I sent one of my customers to see where you went, and you went to 7, Luscombe Street.
CHARLES LANGLEY . I am a butcher, of 3, Simpson Street, Wandsworth Road—on the morning of 22nd October, between eleven and twelve, the prisoner came to my stall in the Wandsworth Road and asked for a pound of 4 1/2 d. pieces, and tendered a half-crown—I gave her 2 s. 1 1/2 d. change, and she went away—I put the half-crown in my pocket—I had no other half-crown there—I looked at it again when I got home, and found it was bad—I put it in the tester and it broke—I put the smallest piece in the fire, and this other piece ( produced) I gave the sergeant—I am quite certain the prisoner is the woman.
ALFRED VAUGHAN . I am a butcher, of 281, Wandsworth Road—on 24th October, about 5.30 in the afternoon, the prisoner came to my stall for half a pound of 4 1/2 d. pieces and tendered this half-crown—I gave her two separate shillings and 1 1/2 d. change and put the half-crown in a jug where there were only coppers—about half an hour after Langley communicated with me, and I then examined the half-crown and found it was bad by indenting it with my teeth—I work at Charles Langley's stall—on 25th October, the next morning, I and Mr. Hubbard saw the prisoner in the Luscombe Road outside a public-house, I recognised her and gave her into the custody of Detective Vagg—I had no conversation with her, the constable had.
ALFRED BEACH ( Policeman W 52). On 25th October the two last witnesses gave the prisoner into my charge for uttering two counterfeit half-crowns on 22nd and 24th October—I said "You will be charged with uttering two counterfeit half-crowns"—she said "I wasn't there, I know nothing about it, I am a respectable married woman"—I took her to the station and she was charged, I was not then present.
AUGUSTUS LABAN . I live at 21, Orris Street, Wandsworth, and am an engineer—on 18th October I was in the Surrey Arms when the prisoner came in for a pint of ale and put down a half-crown, which Mr. Hubbard afterwards passed round the company in the house—I examined it, I
thought it was bad—I put it in my mouth and left teeth marks in it—I am perfectly sure the prisoner is the woman that came in.
WALTER VAGG ( Detective W). I was at the police-station when the prisoner was brought in on 25th October charged by Vaughan and Hubbard with uttering a half-crown to Vaughan and also one to Langley—replied (I took it down at the time) "Well, I was never in the parish, I was never here, I was never out of my home; it is very cruel to bring me here I do not know what a bad half-crown is, I am wrongly here; I am the wrong person, go and bring in the right person, what you are paid to do"—4 d. good Money was found on her—she gave the address, 20, Britannia Place, Bow Lane, Battersea—that is about two and a quarter miles from the Wandsworth Road.
WILLIAM JOHN WEBSTER . This half-crown and a very large portion of another one are both bad, not from the same mould—the teeth generally sink into and make a groove in a bad coin—that is observable on one of these, not much on the-other—you could not do that to a good coin your teeth would come on that as on glass.
The Prisoner's Statement before the Magistrate. "I know nothing of it. I am quite innocent of it. I was at home turning the mangle."
Witnesses for the Defence.
MARY ANN BENNETT . My husband is an engineer's fitter—on 24th October I went into the prisoner's place at 5 o'clock to ask her if she could lend me 1 s. to get my children some tea, and at a quarter-past 5 she lent it me, when her son home came from Price's factory—at a quarter-past 6 she lent my son 4 d. to pay his fare to Vauxhall—at a quarter-past 8 I paid her back the shilling I had borrowed.
Cross-examined. I then lived next door to the prisoner—I might have been two or three minutes after a quarter-past 5—I fix the time by the time her son comes home from Price's, he gets paid at 5 and has five or six minutes' walk from there.
By the COURT. I believe she was at home between a auarter-past 5 and 6—I cannot say she was in and out of my place, but I dont think she was out of the place till after 8—I saw her at different times in my place.
JANE LEGG . I am William Legg's wife—we live at 14, Britannia Place, Battersea, in the same street as Mrs. Bennett—I was in her house on 24th October when the prisoner came in and lent Mrs. Bennett some money a little after five, and at 6 o'clock I was there when she lent Mrs. Bennett's son 4 d.—I was there the whole time, because my little boy was not well, and I was sitting up with it; the prisoner was not there the whole time; she was in and out—she was back at 8 and was paid back again—I know the prisoner through her doing my mangling.
Cross-examined. I saw the prisoner in Mrs. Bennett's house three or four times between 5 and 6, but she did not stop long—I was there nursing my little boy, whom I had taken over to show Mrs. Bennett—I know it was a little after 5 because I have to get my husband's tea at 6, and I got it ready an hour before I went over.
HOLLOWAY. I am a fishmonger and general dealer, of 39, Britannia Place, Battersea—on Friday evening, 24th October about thirty-five minutes to 6, I served the prisoner with some fish—at a quarter to eight she came for some more, and wanted particularly a large one for her husband to take to his work in the morning—I know her husband.
Cross-examined. The prisoner is a customer of mine and does our
mangling very often—I can fix the time because I had been to Price's candle factory—I left there at 25 minutes past 5, and when I opened my door it was half-past 5 by St. Peter's Church opposite my house, and the prisoner came in about five minutes afterwards—I did not go to the police-court because I was not asked—her husband came and asked me if she had been in—I mentioned the hour first to him.
MATTHEW CHARLES BENNETT . I am a labourer of 111, Livingstone Road, near Clapham Junction, Battersea—on 24th October, about 6 o'clock, I was at 19, Britannia Place, where I borrowed 4 d. of the prisoner to pay my fare to Lambeth—I am Mrs. Bennett's son.
JOHN BUDD . I am the prisoner's son—on 24th October I came home with my money at 5 o'clock from Price's candle factory, where I work—the prisoner was at home then, and I saw her there nearly all the evening till I went out at 5.55 or 5.50.
ANNIE CHAPPLE . My husband is an engineer—we live at 7, Luscombe Street, Wandsworth Road—on Wednesday, 22nd October, I was at my sister's, the prisoner's, house, from 11 o'clock till my husband came home to tea about 5.30 or 6—I go by the tram to my sister's; it is a twopenny fare—it might have been a little before 11 when I went; I generally go about that time.
Cross-examined. I go there once or twice a week—I wash and take my little girl to school, and then go—I left home about 9.30, and called on a friend, and got to her about 11—I did not notice the time I got there—I do a little needlework for my sister—she was at home, and made me a cup of tea—she very rarely comes down to me—she has a large family of children—I have lived in the Wandsworth Road for two years, near this butcher's—I have known Langley eight or 10 years—I was in the same shop as he was.
Witness recalled in Reply: —
ALFRED VAUGHAN . On 24th October the prisoner came to my stall about 5.30—I fix the time because the men were just coming out of the works opposite my stall—on 25th October I saw her in Luscombe Street, and communicated with the constable—I had never seen the prisoner before—I don't know Mrs. Chapple.
The prisoner in her defence contended that it was a case of mistaken identity and that she knew nothing about it.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Robert Malcolm Kerr, Esq.
